**Provided properties (all scoped to an honest controller unless noted).** (`background.md` §5.6)

- **P1 -- Validity (Event Authenticity):** "If a correct witness accepts an establishment event, that event was authorized by the controller." Depends A1, A3, A4.
- **P2 -- Consistency (No Forks Under Honest Control):** "Two correct witnesses will not accept different establishment events at the same sequence number." Depends A2, A3, A4, A7. "This is the strongest KAWA property and is *conditional*. It does not hold if the controller is malicious."
- **P3 -- Durability (Immutability After Agreement):** "Once an event has a sufficient agreement, it cannot be removed or replaced." Depends A2, A4, A6. "Durability is local to witnesses and does not imply global convergence."
- **P4 -- Availability:** "Validators can retrieve the KERL even if the controller is offline." Depends A2, A6. "Availability degrades with witness churn or witness set mismanagement."

**Explicitly NOT provided by KAWA.** (`background.md` §5.7)

- **P5 -- Fork Prevention for Malicious Controllers**
- **P6 -- Global Consistency Across Validators**
- **P7 -- Timely Fork Detection**
- **P8 -- Automatic Fork Resolution**

"Any claim implying these properties must introduce **additional mechanisms** (e.g., observers, governance, social response)." (`background.md` §5.7) The division of labor is exact: "KAWA alone determines *local durability*. Observers determine *global detectability*." (`background.md` §5.9) Without observers, KAWA degrades to "Each validator sees *a* consistent history." (`background.md` §5.9)

**Microledgers, not blockchain -- the KAWA framing reviewers most often botch.** This is the shibboleth, and it must be stated explicitly. Blockchains carry a global ledger and global consensus that decides *whether and in what global order an event gets recorded* -- inclusion plus total ordering across all participants. KERI splits that single "blockchain in the sky" into per-identifier microledgers (the KELs), each rooted in its own identifier and held by witnesses and wallets. In KERI the event is **already recorded** by the controller on its own microledger before KAWA runs. KAWA does not ask "should this be recorded?" It asks a different, post-hoc question: is any decentralized actor trying to fork reality *after the fact*? That is detection of equivocation, not prevention of it -- of a piece with survivability-not-invulnerability. (DECENTRALIZATION-PURITY-STEERING §The thesis, human steering note; grounded in KERI spec §KAWA insight)

The spec supplies the mechanical justification for why local ordering suffices where a blockchain would demand global ordering. "because the controller is the sole source of truth for the creation of any and all key events, it alone, is sufficient to order its own key events." Key events are "idempotent authorization operations as opposed to non-idempotent account balance decrement or increment operations. Total or global ordering may be critical for non-idempotency, whereas local ordering may be sufficient for idempotency." (KERI spec, §KAWA insight, L1836) Hence "security guarantees from KAWA may approach that of other BFT algorithms but without their scalability, cost, throughput, or latency limitations." (KERI spec, L1836) A reviewer who asks "where is the chain / why no consensus layer?" has reached for blockchain intuitions in a system that deliberately does not have them, and will be dismissed with justification. (DECENTRALIZATION-PURITY-STEERING §The shibboleth)

**The immunity constraint (the one thing witness thresholds *do* buy).** For N witnesses there is a threshold M < N guaranteeing at most one sufficient agreement (or none), despite a dishonest controller, provided at most F* = N - M are unavailable and F < M are duplicitous -- so "the service may not produce multiple divergent but proper KERL." (KERI spec, L1866-1868) The validator, not the controller, selects M to make the service immune, thereby protecting itself. But this holds only within the stated fault bounds; colluding controller-plus-witnesses violates the <=F assumption, and "KAWA can't protect -- protection then comes from validator-side duplicity detection across a large diverse observer set." (KERI spec, L1874)

## 8. Witnesses, backers, and thresholds

A **witness** is "a server the controller designates to store and serve the KEL." On validating an event it returns a signed receipt; once a threshold (toad) of receipts is collected, "the event is stable." Crucially, "Witnesses make no assertion about identity... That is a fundamentally different role than a CA." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §4) A witness is under the ultimate control of the AID's controller, who may change the witness pool at will. (KERI spec, termdefs) A **backer** is an alternative to a witness, commonly using a distributed ledger to store the KEL. (KERI spec, termdefs)

The witness/toad rules are invariants:

- **TOAD bounds.** A non-empty witness list requires 1 <= toad <= len(wits); an empty list requires toad == 0. (`30-invariants.md` F1; `background.md` §4.2, §5.3)
- **Sufficient agreement = receipts meeting TOAD, not all witnesses.** This is the spec rule (the "threshold of accountable duplicity"): "the controller accepts accountability for an event when any subset M of the N witnesses confirms that event." (KERI spec, L1846, L1850) The reference implementation violates this: keripy's `WitnessReceiptor` waits for ALL witnesses rather than TOAD, "that is a defect, not the invariant," and is the likely root of at least two open issues (`30-invariants.md` F2; `31-landmines.md` L9). That claim was made against keripy `60ab9e08` and is **re-anchored here at `b8f60166b`**, where it still holds: `completed = len(wigers) == len(wits)` with the loop gated on `if len(wigers) != len(wits)`, and no reference to `hab.kever.toader` anywhere in the module (`src/keri/app/agenting.py` L360-361 @`b8f60166b`, read for this chapter) `[K]`.
- **Threshold discipline M > f (A7).** M = 1 provides no fault tolerance and "can be trivially broken by a single malicious witness." (`background.md` §5.3; `dg-c01-claude.md` §3)
- **Witness backers MUST be non-transferable.** Backer AIDs, when serving as witnesses, "MUST be non-transferable, fully qualified public keys" so a validator can verify a witness signature straight from the AID with no witness KEL required. (KERI spec, L353, L1252) Treating this as a discretionary simplification is "a characterization error *about KERI-as-specified*." (`background.md` §2.3) A subtle implementation landmine intersects here: keripy hardcodes `Ed25519N` as the prefix code for any non-transferable AID, which is correct for Ed25519 but wrong for a digest-based scheme; non-transferability in KERI "is expressed by an empty next-key commitment (`ncount=0`, `nsith='0'`), not by a special prefix code." (`31-landmines.md` L1) **Re-anchored at `b8f60166b`**, where the hardcode is still present at both sites -- `if not transferable:` sets `ncount = 0`, `nsith = '0'` and `code = MtrDex.Ed25519N` in `Hab.incept` (L2937-2940), and `nsith = '0'` with the same `code = MtrDex.Ed25519N` in `GroupHab.incept` (L3748-3750) (`src/keri/app/habbing.py` @`b8f60166b`, read for this chapter) `[K]`. This is a hazard for any future digest-based signature scheme, not a defect in flight; see §5 on why no such scheme exists in keripy today.

A standing hygiene rule against a common overreach: "Agreement thresholds do not imply global safety." (`background.md` §4.7) A toad met is local durability achieved, nothing more.

## 9. The observer layer: watchers, jurors, judges, and ambient duplicity detection

Because KAWA provides only local durability, global *detectability* is the job of an observer layer that the protocol enables but does not mandate. The three roles are deliberately separated so that no single observer both detects duplicity and rules on it. (`background.md` §6.2)

- **Watcher.** Monitors identifiers by "Collecting KERLs from multiple sources / Comparing observed histories / Detecting conflicts at identical sequence numbers." It is validator-selected, with "No required coverage or completeness / No protocol-mandated response." (`background.md` §6.2) In the spec's terms, a watcher keeps a copy of a KERL but is *not* designated by the controller; it is "Under control of the VALIDATOR, not the controller," and follows first-seen. (KERI spec, termdefs)
- **Juror (Jury).** "records evidence of duplicity, including: Conflicting events / Witness receipts / Proofs of equivocation." It has "No authority to resolve conflicts" but "Enables post hoc accountability" -- it keeps a Duplicitous Event Log (DEL) and may be a fault-tolerant pool. (`background.md` §6.2; KERI spec, L51, L1858)
- **Judge.** "applies policy or governance rules to: Rank conflicting KERLs / Recommend acceptance or rejection / Trigger recovery or remediation." It is "Policy-driven / Non-universal / May disagree across validators." (`background.md` §6.2)

**"Super" names scope, not a feature set, and it attaches to all three roles.** Where this corpus has said "super watcher" it has been inheriting `wtbo.md`'s usage, in which the word marks the *archival-service* deployment variant of a watcher (`wtbo.md` §5, and §11 below). KERI's maintainers use it differently, and the difference is worth carrying because the loose reading invites exactly the role collapse this section exists to prevent. Answering a draft KID that had defined super watchers by an enumerated feature list — comprehensive polling, push notification, merkle-checkpointed diffs, reputation endpoints — Samuel Smith rejected the framing outright: "the term super watcher seems to be any watcher that has features not in the most basic form of watcher, while many of the features listed above are actually associated with say a Juror and we should then define Jurors and some with a Judge. In my mind what makes a watcher, juror, or judge 'super' is the scope of the KELs it is watching therefore we could have super watchers or super jurors or super judges." Philip Feairheller concurred and supplied the concrete case: "a vLEI Super Watcher would have the scope of all AIDs in that ecosystem." (WebOfTrust/kids#1, 2023-11-05 and 2023-11-06.) **Provenance caveat, since this tier is not in the key above:** these are maintainer statements in a GitHub discussion, on a draft that was never merged, and they sit well below `[N]` — they are recorded here as how the term's own authors use it, not as specification text. Two consequences follow. **Super juror** and **super judge** are coherent terms this corpus has otherwise had no room for, so a deployment presented only as a "super watcher" may be doing juror or judge work under a name that conceals the role separation above; ask which of the three it performs. And *scope* is the criterion that actually does the work in the centralization argument of §11 — what makes the GLEIF super watcher a trust concentration is that one operator's view spans an entire ecosystem, not that it keeps an archive.

The detection mechanic is simple and trust-free: if a watcher "asks two witnesses for the head of the same identifier and gets different hash values, it has detected *duplicity*... The watcher can broadcast both events as cryptographic proof of fraud. That proof doesn't require trusting any authority's interpretation -- only the mathematics of the signatures." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §4)

**Ambient duplicity detection** is the emergent, non-guaranteed property that equivocation "becomes observable *somewhere* in the network, given sufficient observer activity and information flow. This is **not** guaranteed by protocol mechanics." It depends on observer presence, diligence, information overlap, and time. (`background.md` §6.3) The spec ties this to the asymmetry it creates for the defender: "a successful dead attack requires the isolation of a validator from ambient sources of the KERL... isolation... may be prohibitively expensive. Consequently, ambient verifiability provides asymmetry between the attacker and the defender in favor of the defender." (KERI spec, L1884)

The observer-enabled properties are themselves conditional: **O1** Duplicity Detection (depends A8, A9, A10; "may be delayed or incomplete"); **O2** Evidence-Based Accountability ("Observed duplicity can be proven to third parties"; depends A1, A11; "Proof does not imply remediation"); **O3** Validator-Informed Choice ("Choices may diverge permanently"). (`background.md` §6.5) And what observers do *not* provide is enumerated: "Global convergence / Guaranteed detection / Timely detection / Automatic resolution / Enforcement against controllers or witnesses." The one-line summary is doctrine: "**Observers increase *visibility*, not *control*.**" (`background.md` §6.6)

The papers add a governance-flavored distinction that reviewers coming from the browser/CA world need: a watcher makes an *observation*, not an *enforcement* decision. "A browser vendor that distrusts a CA is making an enforcement decision -- exercising judgment with immediate global consequences... A KERI watcher that detects duplicity is making an observation -- surfacing cryptographic evidence that the relying party confirms independently." (`wtbo.md` §4) "Watchers cannot unilaterally 'distrust' an identifier the way a browser root program can distrust a CA. The worst a dishonest watcher can do is stay silent about duplicity it has seen -- a risk addressed by running your own watcher or consulting multiple independent ones." (`wtbo.md` §4) The recommended default is the *local-resolver* pattern: a watcher "can be (and by default usually is) as simple as an in-process component in a verifier's own software stack that queries multiple witnesses directly before reporting any result." (`wtbo.md` §5)

A role-boundary invariant guards against a subtle confusion: **a witness is NOT a watcher.** A witness may serve a delegate's KEL without the delegator's seal or KEL; a validator "must independently obtain the delegator's KEL (OOBI) before validating." (`30-invariants.md` D3; `31-landmines.md` L4) Code that assumes a witness performs watcher functionality will fail.

**Contested / immature -- flag for reviewers.** The observer layer is where the corpus's own honest criticism lands. It is the *only* residual that survives objective-function realignment across the six Del Giudice claims, and it is a deployment-maturity gap, not a protocol flaw. "A KERI deployment without robust watchers and governance is not a secure system." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §5) The CISO reading is blunt: treat "observer/watcher infrastructure as a first-class security control, not an optional add-on," and expect early-deployment detection to be "delayed rather than immediate," "probabilistic rather than deterministic," "coverage-dependent," and "action-dependent" on governance. (dg-c0X §7 non-normative notes) Real failure modes named: correlated witness failure (same cloud provider), weak thresholds chosen for convenience, siloed or lazy watchers, and alert fatigue. (`cr-ad-trust.md` §5; dg-c0X §7)

## 10. Local vs global validity

The deepest departure from blockchain intuition is that KERI does not promise validators will ever agree. Validity is *local to a validator* and observer-dependent. The corpus disambiguates four notions of convergence, and a reviewer must keep them apart:

- **Local Consistency** -- "Its accepted KERL is internally consistent / No conflicts observed within its view." "Local consistency is always achievable." (`background.md` §7.4.1)
- **Global Convergence** -- "All validators accept the same KERL for an identifier." "**KERI does not guarantee global convergence.**" (`background.md` §7.4.2)
- **Eventual Awareness** -- "Conflicts become observable to some validators over time." "This is possible, not guaranteed." (`background.md` §7.4.3)
- **Permanent Divergence** -- "Validators accept different KERLs indefinitely." "**Permanent divergence is an allowed outcome.**" (`background.md` §7.4.4)

Safety and liveness are correspondingly reframed. "Safety in KERI means: Validators do not accept *invalid* histories." (`background.md` §7.5.1) It does **not** mean validators agree on a single history. "Liveness in KERI means: Honest controllers can continue to append events." (`background.md` §7.5.2) It does **not** mean prompt observation or conflict resolution. Validators are autonomous: they "do not share state by default," "May have disjoint trust relationships," and "May never interact directly." (`background.md` §7.1)

The spec states the local-validity rule as a validator obligation: "An honest validator MUST trust when there is no evidence of duplicity and MUST NOT trust when there is any evidence of duplicity unless and until the duplicity has been reconciled." (KERI spec, L53) Whether a validator treats its own first-seen copy as authoritative is "at the validator's discretion." (KERI spec, L1842) The reconciliation model treats the KEL as a DAG whose undisputed path is the *trunk*; if validators cannot universally find the same undisputed path, the KEL is *irreconcilable* and is not trusted. (KERI spec, §Reconciliation, L1788-1790)

It is essential to note that permanent divergence is a *protocol-level property*, not merely a deployment artifact: it is "permitted *even with observers present*." (`dg-c03-claude.md` §3) Observers can make divergence *visible*; they cannot make it *converge*. This is the fact that a blockchain-trained reviewer finds most surprising and the corpus most insistent upon.

## 11. The Del Giudice claim bindings (DG-C01..C06)

The 2025 ETH Zurich Master's thesis by Del Giudice, *A Security Analysis of KERI*, is "the most rigorous formal analysis of KERI's consensus mechanics" but "not peer-reviewed research." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §5) Its six normalized claims are the canonical checklist for what KERI does and does not guarantee, and each has been adjudicated against KERI's stated objective function. The meta-pattern is the most useful single takeaway: every apparent weakness resolves into one of three buckets -- a deliberate non-goal, a proof-scope limitation, or a deployment/ecosystem-maturity gap -- and the only genuinely surviving residuals are about observer-infrastructure immaturity, never about the protocol. (`background.md` §8.2, meta-pattern)

- **DG-C01 -- KAWA provides fork-resistance for honest controllers (under strong assumptions).** KAWA gives validity, consistency, and reliability for honest controllers during establishment events, under BFT-style quorum sizing and bounded faults. Assumptions: A1, A2, A3, A4, A7. Verdict: **faithful within scope; a positive claim, not a criticism.** Risks are all reader-side over-generalization: assuming it extends to all deployments, under-appreciating the detection layer, or assuming BFT thresholds are mandatory. (`dg-c01-claude.md`)

- **DG-C02 -- KERI does not prevent duplicity by malicious controllers.** At best duplicity is detectable via evidence propagation; neither prevention nor timely detection is guaranteed. Verdict: **faithful to the mechanics but misaligned with KERI's objectives** -- it "criticizes KERI for lacking a capability... that is **explicitly outside KERI's design goals**," treating a non-goal as a failure and making KERI "appear **less secure than it is**." The one *legitimate* residual that survives realignment: detection is delegated to an *optional* observer layer, which is "a critique of *deployment readiness and ecosystem maturity*, not of KERI's protocol design." (`dg-c02-claude.md` §2, §5, §6) The thesis's own honest phrasing: "We don't have any guarantees however on if and when this duplicity will be exposed." (Ch.4 §4.1 p.38)

- **DG-C03 -- Validators may accept conflicting KERLs indefinitely.** Different validators may accept different valid KERLs, and divergence may persist indefinitely with no protocol-level resolution -> "not duplicity-resistant but only duplicity-evident." Assumptions: A12, A13. Verdict: the **descriptive portion is faithful** (permanent divergence is a permitted outcome, §7.4.4); the evaluative "only" risks importing an invulnerability expectation. "**No criticism survives objective-function alignment**" when KERI is evaluated as survivability-oriented. Note the sharp point: divergence is a protocol property, "permitted *even with observers present*." (`dg-c03-claude.md` §2, §3, §6)

- **DG-C04 -- Recovery rotation blocks proof of a single authoritative KERL.** Under recovery rotation, correct witnesses may sign both an attacker's fraudulent event and the legitimate owner's recovery event at the same sequence number, "because KERI prioritizes the ability to reclaim a compromised identity over strict uniqueness of history at every moment." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §5) The consequence: "validators may see multiple mathematically valid KELs for the same identifier, and no cryptographic mechanism alone can determine which is legitimate." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §5) Assumptions: none beyond the base model -- the fork occurs *even when all actors behave correctly*. Verdict: technically correct; it "buys real-world recoverability at the cost of formal elegance in edge cases." Treating "inability to prove uniqueness" as insecurity is a category error, because "KERI's security model is **evidence-based** rather than **proof-based**"; formal uniqueness is unprovable, but "**duplicity remains detectable**." (`dg-c04-claude.md` §5.1) The alternative would be "an identity you cannot recover from compromise without involving a trusted third party -- which brings back exactly the administrative dependency KERI was designed to escape." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §5)

- **DG-C05 -- Practical mitigation requires governance and monitoring.** Effective mitigation "likely requires governance, monitoring, or centralized archival services (e.g., a 'super watcher'), which introduce additional trust assumptions." Assumptions: A8-A11. Verdict: **faithful and survivability-aligned** -- "not a failure of KERI's design but an acknowledgment that survivability properties emerge from **system composition** (protocol + observers + governance), not protocol alone." Caveat: "additional trust assumptions" is better read as operational dependencies and incentive/coverage assumptions -- except the super-watcher case, which *is* genuine trust centralization. (`dg-c05-claude.md` §2, §5, §6) The GLEIF super-watcher is the live example: "This works, but it reintroduces a centralized trust dependency that KERI was designed to avoid." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §5)

- **DG-C06 -- Strong security results depend on narrow scope plus strong assumptions.** The strongest proofs hold only for honest controllers, bounded witness faults, reliable communication, and uncompromised pre-committed rotation keys; malicious-controller behavior breaks termination/liveness *in the proofs*. Verdict: **faithful as a statement about the proofs' scope**, but misleading if read as "KERI requires honest controllers." The key correction: observer mechanisms are "explicitly excluded from Del Giudice's formal analysis" (`background.md` §8.3) yet are "central to KERI's security model." Malicious controllers "trigger detection, not failure," and honest-controller is "a **proof technique assumption**... not a KERI deployment assumption." (`dg-c06-claude.md` §3, §4, §5)

## 12. What is established, what is contested, and the load-bearing assumptions

**Established (uncontested within the corpus, stated identically across security analysis, papers, spec, and the reference-implementation knowledge base):** the survivability objective function; detection-over-prevention; duplicity-evidence as the characteristic property; the zero-trust/malicious-controller stance and its status as an explicit non-goal to *prevent* a fully malicious controller; the pre-rotation firewall and the survivable-vs-catastrophic asymmetry between current-key and next-key compromise; KAWA's provided properties P1-P4 and its non-properties P5-P8; the local/observer-dependent nature of validity and the permissibility of permanent divergence; and the observers-increase-visibility-not-control boundary.

**Contested or genuinely limiting (the residuals that survive scrutiny):** exactly one class of criticism survives objective-function alignment, and it is not about the protocol -- it is that KERI's *detection* depends on an *optional, still-immature* observer/watcher/governance layer (the surviving residual of DG-C02, and the whole of DG-C05). "A KERI deployment without robust watchers and governance is not a secure system." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §5) The super-watcher pattern relieves coverage gaps but reintroduces genuine centralization (`cr-ad-trust.md` §5; DG-C05). Separately, the formal literature is thin: the strongest analysis is a non-peer-reviewed Master's thesis, and it explicitly excludes the observer layer that KERI's security model most depends on. (`wtbo.md` §5; `background.md` §8.3) None of this is a design defect; the corpus is candid that these are "constraints of *immaturity*, not of *architecture*." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §6)

**A companion problem about evidence standards, which the corpus raised against itself this month.** The thinness of the formal literature has a second edge worth naming here, because an author in this corpus named it about his own work. A paper of his once described the review behind its central estimate as having been submitted "to two independent adversarial expert reviews, one in vision science, one in security usability" (`m-glance.md` L66-67 @`32d2d43`). The current revision retracts that: those passes were "conducted by AI models prompted to argue from a named lens, not by human domain experts", and "They are a structured way to attack one's own assumptions, not independent validation" (`m-glance.md` §1, L71-74 @`181569b64`). A sibling paper made the same move in the same window, restating its own status downward to "*theoretically motivated, adversarially self-reviewed, and empirically untested*" (`amp-diff.md` §6.3, L405 @`181569b64`). Neither paper is about KERI and neither is cited anywhere else in this chapter; the finding is methodological, and it lands squarely on how this chapter's own material was produced. A structured adversarial pass -- a persona panel, a red-team prompt, a lens-driven critique -- attacks your own assumptions, and it produces exactly the sort of confident, well-organized output that reads like peer review and is not. Its evidentiary weight sits below a non-peer-reviewed Master's thesis, not beside it. That bears directly on the six per-claim adjudications in §11: their filenames (`dg-c01-claude.md` and siblings) mark them as model-authored, which makes them a structured self-attack on the thesis rather than an independent check of it, and they should be read and cited as such. The DG verdicts remain the most careful reading of Del Giudice this corpus has. They are not a second opinion.

**Load-bearing assumptions a reviewer must never grant for free:** A3 (honest controller -- a proof convenience, not a field premise); A5 (observer presence -- optional and MUST NOT be silently assumed); A7 (threshold discipline M > f -- M = 1 is trivially breakable); and the entire A8-A11 quartet (observer coverage, diligence, information flow, evidence integrity -- *none* enforced by KERI). Any claim that duplicity *will* be detected, *promptly* detected, or *automatically* resolved is smuggling one or more of these in, and violates the P5-P8 non-properties. Any claim of a single global truth for an identifier contradicts the local-validity model outright.

## 13. Where KERI corrects the outsider's prior

Several review reflexes are, in the corpus's own terms, category errors. Named explicitly so a reviewer can catch them in their own reasoning:

- **"Security means invulnerability; prevent all forks."** Category error. KERI is survivability-oriented; forks by malicious controllers are anticipated disturbances, and criticizing the lack of prevention is "criticizing a smoke detector for not preventing fires." (`background.md` §4.5.1; `dg-c02-claude.md` §5)
- **"You need a blockchain / global consensus / global total ordering."** Explicit non-goal. (`background.md` §1.3) Key events are idempotent authorization operations, so local ordering suffices; the event is *already recorded* on its microledger, and KAWA does post-hoc fork detection, not consensus on inclusion. (KERI spec §KAWA insight, L1836; DECENTRALIZATION-PURITY-STEERING)
- **"A threshold of witnesses equals global safety."** No. "Agreement thresholds do not imply global safety." (`background.md` §4.7) KAWA gives local durability; global detectability needs observers.
- **"All validators converge on one truth (finality)."** No. Permanent divergence is allowed; safety does not mean agreement on a single history; validators may never interact. (`background.md` §7.4, §7.5, §7.1)
- **"Detection is inferior to prevention."** A smuggled invulnerability premise; the argument must justify *why* prevention is required given KERI's threat model, and it never does. (`dg-c02-claude.md` §5)
- **"If a formal proof assumes an honest controller, the system is insecure against dishonest ones."** The honest-controller premise is a proof simplification; dishonesty triggers detection, not failure. (`dg-c06-claude.md` §3, §5)
- **"Unprovable uniqueness means broken."** KERI is evidence-based, not proof-based; recovery rotation deliberately trades provable uniqueness for real-world recoverability without a trusted third party. (`dg-c04-claude.md`; `cr-ad-trust.md` §5)
- **"The root of trust must be an authority (CA/administrator)."** KERI's root is the identifier's own key-event history, replayable offline "without querying any authority." Witnesses "make no assertion about identity." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §3-§4; `00-lens.md` claim 1)

The method-level tell that ties these together: the analysis template *forces* the analyst to name the objective function first and to restate any claim verbatim, precisely because outsider critiques smuggle in unstated invulnerability goals. "you MUST restate the claim verbatim... If you cannot do so, stop and ask." (`standard-analysis.md` §0) A reviewer who cannot state which objective function a criticism assumes has not yet earned the right to make it.

---

# 03 — Key Management and Identifier Lifecycle

## Thesis

An Autonomic Identifier is not a name that points at a key; it is the head of a signed, append-only history of its own key state, and everything an adversarial reviewer needs to understand about KERI's key management follows from taking that sentence literally. The identifier is derived from its inception event, so it cannot be separated from the key material that first controlled it; authority moves forward through rotation events that are cryptographically chained to a commitment made *before* the current keys were ever exposed; and the single most important structural fact -- the one that distinguishes KERI from every certificate, token, and DID method a reviewer is likely to bring priors about -- is that control authority is split in two. Signing authority (the current keys) does the day-to-day work and is deliberately treated as expendable; rotation authority (the *next* keys, published only as a hash) is the crown jewel, held offline, and its compromise is the one unrecoverable event in the whole system. This section walks the lifecycle in the order the protocol enforces it: inception, the transferable/non-transferable fork, the establishment-versus-interaction distinction, pre-rotation and the family of rotations built on it, multisig, cooperative delegation, witness and backer designation, recovery (superseding) rotation, the as-of-anchor rule that defeats retrograde attacks, and the KERIA/Signify edge-versus-agent custody split that puts all of this into production. Throughout, it separates what the keripy/keria/signify code actually enforces from what the prose merely describes, because in a security review the gap between the two is where the defects live.

## 0. How to read this chapter: the markers, and what each one is pinned to

Most of this chapter is `[N]` and most of its code claims are `[K]`, so the markers appear only where the distinction does work — principally in the delegation section, where the two specification branches now say materially different things about one config trait.

- **[N]** — **Normative.** Present in the line the community is actively standardizing: KERI specification `main` @ `fbdd4a615` (2026-08-28). An unmarked specification citation in this chapter means `[N]`.
- **[N1.1]** — present in the KERI specification's **`v1.1`** branch @ `3753f3797` (2026-08-26) and **not** in `main`. The two branches have diverged and neither is an ancestor of the other, so `[N1.1]` here is not "`main` plus more" and a claim cannot be promoted to `[N]` on the expectation that the branch merges forward. CESR-branch material carries the same marker against CESR `v1.1` @ `65fd7518b`, named where it is used.
- **[K]** — **implementation behavior at a named commit.** Several pins, and the differences between them matter. keripy `main` @ `b8f60166b` (2026-09-14, `__version__` `2.1.0-dev1`) is the note pin; but `core/eventing.py`, `core/coring.py`, `vdr/eventing.py`, `app/habbing.py`, `app/keeping.py` and `core/signing.py` — which are where nearly every code claim in this chapter lives — were **last read at keripy `3a8e01ae`** (2026-07-17) and were not re-read at `b8f60166b`. Their line hints and their behavior are pinned to `3a8e01ae`, and this chapter says so rather than inheriting the newer pin it did not earn. KERIA is pinned at `7e685edaa` (2026-09-11) and signify-ts at `ffba8406e` (2026-09-10). Where a claim was re-read at `b8f60166b`, it says so at the point of use.
- **Knowledge-base material** — the invariants `30-invariants.md` and the landmines `31-landmines.md`, cited throughout as `L1`, `L6`, `C1`, `H1` and so on — comes from a **private, unpublished** contributor knowledge base pinned at `9489ac9bd` (2026-07-02), **whose own code baseline is keripy `60ab9e08`, 493 commits behind `b8f60166b`**. Every landmine in this chapter is therefore a `[K]`-class observation about a superseded dev line. None of them is known to be wrong; none may be read as a statement about current keripy without re-checking the file. The knowledge base's own banner claim that all twenty landmines still hold was verified at `60ab9e08` and has at least one known counterexample since, so this chapter states each landmine it uses individually rather than repeating that claim.

Unmarked claims are this chapter's synthesis. Sources are mined in `raw/01-keri-spec.md`, `raw/02-cesr-spec.md`, `raw/05-papers-priority.md`, `raw/08-keripy-kb.md`, `raw/09-keripy-code.md` and `raw/10-keria-signify.md`.

**Line hints re-anchored in this pass.** Every KERI specification line hint below 2838 is identical at `fbdd4a615` to what it was at the corpus's previous `4df80aa` stamp — the only edits below the OOBI section are two same-line typo fixes — so every specification hint in this chapter except two was re-anchored by a verified file-wide mapping and did not move. The two exceptions are the BADA citations in section 11, which did move and are corrected in place. The `v1.1` hints used in section 8 are `v1.1`-native and are identical to `main`'s below line 475. Hints into keripy modules were **not** re-anchored, because those modules were not re-read; they remain hints as of keripy `3a8e01ae`.

## 1. The lifecycle in one frame: the KEL and its event types

The Key Event Log (KEL) is "a Verifiable data structure that is a backward and forward chained, signed, append-only log of key events for an AID. The first entry... must be the one and only Inception event." (KERI spec, termdefs; also spec-body.md L176) "Each KEL is somewhat like a 'blockchain' that manages the key state for one and only one AID." (KERI spec, L31) It is doubly hash-chained: backward via the digest of the previous event (`p` field), and forward via the pre-rotation commitment to the next keys' digests. (KERI spec, L31, L176)

Key events come in five ilks: inception (`icp`), rotation (`rot`), interaction (`ixn`), delegated inception (`dip`), and delegated rotation (`drt`). (KERI spec, L1748) The primary papers reduce this to three families for the non-delegated case: "inception (`icp`), rotation (`rot`), interaction (`ixn`) (anchors data/seals without changing keys)." (`keri-primer.md` §2, in raw/05 L130) "The head of the log *is* the current state, and anyone can verify it offline, at any time." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §4) That last property -- offline, authority-free verifiability -- is the whole point: a verifier establishes who controls an AID "by replaying its KEL from inception, not by querying any trusted directory or CA." (`00-lens.md` claim 1)

The **key state** is what a replay computes: current public keys plus signing threshold, pre-rotated next-key digests plus next threshold, the witness list plus witness threshold, and configuration traits. It is time-dependent, established at inception, and evolves only via establishment events. (KERI spec, termdefs "Key state") In keripy this state object is `Kever` (the key-state machine) with its persisted `KeyStateRecord`; `Kever.incept` sets `verfers`, `tholder` (signing threshold), `ndigers` (next-key digests), and witnesses "from the event body itself." (`core/eventing.py:2323`; raw/09 §1)

## 2. Inception: how an AID is born

Inception is the genesis event, and its constraints are the tightest in the protocol because everything downstream depends on them.

- **Sequence number must be zero.** keripy: `if self.sner.positive: raise ValidationError(f"Nonzero sn... in inception event")` (`eventing.py:2335-2336`; KERI spec L323). There MUST be exactly one inception per AID: "There MUST be only one Establishment event that is an Inception event. All subsequent Establishment events MUST be Rotation events." (KERI spec, L1378) A second sn=0 with a different SAID is treated as likely-duplicitous (escrow and raise). (`30-invariants.md` B1; `31-landmines.md` L8)
- **The identifier is the digest of the inception event.** For a self-addressing (transferable) AID, the `d` (SAID) field and the `i` (identifier) field "MUST have the same value and same derivation code," and validation requires `d == i`. (KERI spec, L1380-1381) keripy computes the SAID by the dummy-fill method: copy the event, fill the `d` field with the non-Base64 dummy char `#` sized to the digest, serialize, digest, and overwrite the field with the result (`coring.py:4058-4096`, `Saider._derive`; `serdering.py:Serder.makify`; `30-invariants.md` B5). "Changing field order/serialization changes the SAID." (`30-invariants.md` B5) This is the primer's "crystallized fact": the identifier is a tamper-evident commitment to the exact bytes of the event that created it. (`keri-primer.md` §3.2)
- **The signing threshold size cannot exceed the number of keys:** `if len(self.verfers) < self.tholder.size` -> "Invalid sith" (`eventing.py:2340`).
- **Configuration traits are fixed at inception and constrain the whole KEL.** The Establishment-Only (`EO`) trait forbids interaction events, so every event must be signed by first-time one-time pre-rotated keys and signing-key reuse becomes impossible; a validator MUST drop non-establishment events for such an AID. (KERI spec, L377) The Do-Not-Delegate (`DND`) trait means a validator MUST drop delegated events whose delegator carries it. (KERI spec, L379)

The derivation is the fork in the road. The **basic** derivation embeds the public key directly in the prefix (CESR code `B` = "Ed25519 verification key non-transferable"; `1AAA`/`1AAC`/`1AAI` for non-transferable ECDSA/Ed448/secp256r1). The **self-addressing** derivation uses a digest of the inception event (code `E` = Blake3-256). (`coring.py:244-294`; raw/09 §1) "The code prefix (the leading selector char) tells the verifier the crypto suite -- crypto agility is structural, not negotiated." (raw/09 §1) Only prefixive codes may be AIDs: `Prefixer.__init__` raises `InvalidCodeError` if the code is not in `PreDex` (`coring.py:3830-3831`).

## 3. Transferable versus non-transferable AIDs

This is a fork with sharp, load-bearing consequences, and it is a place where a reviewer's instinct is frequently wrong.

A **non-transferable** AID cannot rotate. "A basic SCID... does not support Rotation... and therefore MUST be abandoned once the controlling private key becomes weakened or compromised." (KERI spec, L107) The papers put it plainly: basic AIDs are "suitable only for ephemeral, short-lived use cases." (`keri-primer.md` §2.1; raw/05 L98) The identifier *is* the key digest, so changing the key means abandoning the identifier and all reputation attached to it.

A **transferable** AID survives rotation precisely because it is derived from the entire inception event -- initial keys plus the pre-rotation commitment -- rather than from a bare key. "The identifier string remains constant even as the keys change... Identity survives key rotation." (`keri-primer.md` §2.1; raw/06 §1.1) This is the tetrad: for transferable AIDs a fourth element, the KEL, joins the triad of identifier/keypairs/controllers to "persist bindings across rotation," and "The essence of the KERI protocol is a strongly bound tetrad." (KERI spec, L162-182)

**The correction to the outsider prior.** Non-transferability is *not* expressed by a special prefix code as a general matter; it is expressed by an empty next-key commitment. "Non-transferability in KERI is expressed by an empty next-key commitment (`ncount=0`, `nsith='0'`), not by a special prefix code -- except where a basic non-transferable code like `Ed25519N` legitimately carries the key itself." (`31-landmines.md` L1) keripy enforces the empty-commitment side of this hard: a non-transferable prefix MUST have empty next digests, empty witness list, and empty anchored data at inception -- `if not self.prefixer.transferable and ndigs:` -> ValidationError "Invalid inception next digest list not empty for non-transferable prefix" (`eventing.py:2352-2355`, `:2362`, `:2383`; `30-invariants.md` C4). Rotation is forbidden when there is no prior next commitment: `if not self.ndigers: raise ValidationError("Attempted rotation for nontransferable prefix...")` (`eventing.py:2650-2653`). Rotating to a null next-key list on a transferable AID is itself the abandonment signal: "no more key events MUST be allowed in its KEL." (KERI spec, L340, L174)

**The landmine here is real, and its scope is narrower than it used to read.** keripy's `Hab.incept` (and `GroupHab.incept`) hardcodes `code = MtrDex.Ed25519N` for *any* non-transferable AID regardless of the actual signing-key type (`habbing.py:2931`, `:3728`). This is correct for Ed25519 but wrong for a post-quantum non-transferable AID, which needs a digest-based prefix. (`31-landmines.md` L1, at knowledge-base pin `9489ac9bd`, code baseline keripy `60ab9e08`) A reviewer of post-quantum work should treat this as a known defect, not a template.

**What that landmine is *not* evidence of.** The knowledge base states L1 as advice about work in flight — it calls the hardcode "directly blocking" a Falcon implementation — and that framing is out of date. A case-insensitive search of `src/keri` at keripy `b8f60166b` for FN-DSA, ML-DSA, SLH-DSA, Dilithium, Falcon, SPHINCS, Kyber and ML-KEM returns **only** the `falcon` HTTP framework in `app/httping.py` and `app/indirecting.py`; `core/coring.py`'s `MatterCodex` has no post-quantum entries at all. (raw/09 §10 item 3, read at `b8f60166b`) Nothing is blocked, because nothing has been written. The knowledge base is candid about why: its own plan asks which of two conflicting in-repo Falcon design documents is canonical — fixed-size signatures with `1AAQ`/`1AAR` and no digest AIDs, against variable-size signatures with digest AIDs — and defers the work with "resolve the two conflicting Falcon spec docs first." (`PLAN.md` §7, §9, at `9489ac9bd`) So the *discipline* here survives intact and is the right thing to carry forward; the *urgency* does not, and a chapter that states the design question as settled would be inventing an answer neither source has.

**Witnesses must be non-transferable.** When a backer AID serves as a witness it "MUST be non-transferable, fully qualified public keys" so a validator can verify a witness receipt straight from the AID with no witness KEL needed. (KERI spec, L353, L1252) The security analysis is emphatic that this is a *specification* constraint, not a discretionary simplification: treating it as optional is "a characterization error about KERI-as-specified." (`background.md` §2.3; raw/07 §6)

## 4. Establishment versus interaction events

The establishment/non-establishment split is the axis on which authority turns.

- **Establishment events** (`icp`, `rot`, `dip`, `drt`) establish or change key state. **Non-establishment events** (`ixn`) anchor data via seals but change no key state. (KERI spec, L1751-1752)
- **Establishment events gate authority.** Current keys always come from the last establishment event; a message (including an `ixn`) is authorized only if its signatures satisfy that establishment event's current `Tholder` threshold. (`30-invariants.md` B4; `background.md` §3.4)
- **Interaction events cannot change keys and cannot supersede anything** (see recovery rotation below). Their job is to bind external data to key state at a point in the sequence via seals, which is what gives an anchored signature its permanence. Sealing is "an indirect signature that persists across key-state change... validity persists in spite of later changes to the key state... essential for unbounded term but verifiable issuances." (KERI spec, L1280, L395)

**The consequence that two implementations got wrong in the same month, so it is worth stating rather than leaving implicit.** If establishment events are the only events that change key state, then **the key state in force at a position in a KEL is the one established by the last establishment event at or before that position, and any interaction events interleaved since do not move it.** "Current key state" and "the state at the latest event" are therefore not the same thing, and in a KEL with any interaction traffic at all — which is the normal case — the latest event is usually an `ixn`, which establishes nothing. Anything that has to name *which key state* a signature is to be checked against must name the last establishment event; naming the latest event of any kind names an event that carries no keys.

In September 2026 keripy and KERIA converged on exactly that rule, independently, six days apart, from opposite sides of the same wire. On the **receiver** side, keripy's `kraming.py` had been gating attached transferable signature groups on `number.sn != kever.sner.num or sdiger.qb64 != kever.serder.said` — the latest event — and at `b8f60166b` the same gate reads `if (number.sn != kever.lastEst.s or sdiger.qb64 != kever.lastEst.d): continue`, with the reasoning written into the module docstring: "Signing keys change only at establishment events, so the key state ref names the last establishment event rather than the last event of any kind." `[K]` (`kraming.py` module docstring :70-75 and `Kramer._verifyAttachedSigs` :706-707, at `b8f60166b`) On the **sender** side, KERIA's `endrole` and `locscheme` reply handlers were building their signature group from `hab.kever.sn` and `hab.kever.serder.said`, and now build it from `coring.Seqner(sn=hab.kever.lastEst.s)` and `coring.Saider(qb64=hab.kever.lastEst.d)`. `[K]` (`keria/src/keria/app/aiding.py:1665-1666` and `:1766-1767`, at `7e685edaa`; changed by `b57780a`, 2026-09-07) Different authors, different repositories, different roles, both arriving from a failure rather than from a specification — because no specification states the rule. Fourteen other sites in KERIA already had it right, which is what makes the two that did not read as drift rather than as a design disagreement. Chapter 09 owns the KRAM machinery this surfaced in; what belongs here is the lifecycle fact underneath it, and the observation that a rule living only in a docstring is a rule that can drift without anything catching it.

Why the distinction matters for security: exposing a signing key repeatedly (as interaction events do) widens the window in which a stolen current key can be used, but it never risks rotation authority, which the next-key commitment already protects. An AID that wants to eliminate even the signing-reuse window declares the `EO` trait and signs *every* event with fresh one-time pre-rotated keys. (KERI spec, L377) A seal's ordering is itself meaningful -- "ordering of seals = verifiable ordering of endorsements." (KERI spec, L395)

## 5. Pre-rotation: the firewall and the crown jewel

Pre-rotation is the mechanism that makes a transferable identifier survivable, and its full security philosophy is developed in Section 02 (survivability, the detection-and-rotation race, the catastrophic-versus-survivable asymmetry). Here the concern is the mechanics -- exactly what is committed, exactly what is checked, and exactly where the reference code can go wrong.

**What is committed.** Every establishment event names the *current* signing keys in cleartext (the `k` list) but the *next* keys only as digests (the `n` list). "control authority is split between keypairs that hold signing authority and keypairs that hold rotation authority." (KERI spec, §Pre-rotation, L1364) The knowledge base compresses the doctrine to a slogan: "Pre-rotation is the firewall; the next key is the crown jewel." (`00-lens.md` claim 4) The next key itself "stays offline, in cold storage" (`cr-ad-trust.md` §4); the primer's operational rule is that "The next (rotation) key must be managed differently/independently from the current key -- offline, air-gapped, cold storage, different device. This independence is what makes pre-rotation a real firewall." (raw/05 L95; `keri-primer.md` §2.3)

**Why it is post-quantum by construction.** The commitment is a hash of a public key, and hashes resist quantum attack far better than signatures: Grover's algorithm gives only a quadratic speedup, not Shor's exponential one, so a 256-bit hash "retains roughly 128 bits of post-quantum security." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §3-§4) The KERI specification makes the same argument in its own words: a post-quantum attack that could invert the one-way public-key generation "must first invert the digest of the public key using non-quantum computation. Pre-quantum cryptographic strength is, therefore, not weakened post-quantum." (KERI spec, L1711-1713) A quantum adversary who breaks the current signing key "gains the ability to forge interaction events -- but not to rotate, because the pre-rotation commitment is protected by a hash." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §4) Hardman's royal-succession analogy is the clearest statement of the timing: "commitment precede [sic] exposure... The quantum-vulnerable public rotation key is only revealed at the exact moment it is used, after which authority has already passed to a new, secret successor. The attacker is always one step behind." (`kspqs.md` §Recovery and Invalidation, L88 @`181569b64`; the missing *s* is the source's and is preserved so the quote can be found) The pre-rotated keys "serve as first-time, one-time, and only-time rotation keys in the next rotation operation." (KERI spec, L1628)

**Three boundaries on that paragraph, none of which the paragraph states on its own.** The hash-firewall claim itself is unmoved and remains `[N]`: nothing read this cycle contradicts it. What has moved is what may be claimed *around* it.

First, the "always one step behind" framing is about *rotation* authority, and the same author qualifies it sharply when writing about *signing* authority: "the hash protects the *next* key, never the one currently exposed, so safety rests on the controller detecting compromise and rotating before an adversary can exploit what the rotation reveals." (`wtbo.md` §4, L99 @`181569b64`) These are not contradictory — they describe two different keys — but read alone the first states an unconditional guarantee and the second states a race with a live window, and the second is the honest one to lead with. Where this corpus states the strength of pre-rotation, it cites `wtbo` §4 L99; where it uses the `kspqs` analogy, as here, the qualifier travels with it.

Second, the CESR specification's `v1.1` branch has now conceded the same point in normative prose, which is the first place a specification in this family says it: hiding pre-rotated keys behind digests "protects the next key pair but does not protect the currently exposed one," and pre-rotation and post-quantum signature primitives are "complementary, not alternatives; pre-rotation continues to provide defense in depth for whichever signature scheme is in use." `[N1.1]` (CESR spec `v1.1` §Post-Quantum Cryptographic Primitives, L1553 @`65fd7518b`) A protocol "that must resist a quantum adversary in the present tense needs post-quantum signature Primitives directly." (same) This is `[N1.1]` and must not be read as `[N]` — the standardizing CESR branch has no counterpart section at all. Its effect on this chapter is to close the gap between the spec family's register and `wtbo`'s: pre-rotation is defense in depth for the signature scheme in use, not a substitute for one.

Third, the "effective 128 bits" number is contested by a body the argument's own author cites. ENISA's draft guidance "does ask for 384-bit hash outputs where quantum security is sought, on the ground that a 256-bit output's quantum security level could arguably drop below 128 bits." (`kspqs.md` §The Pre-Rotation Mechanism, L72 @`181569b64`) Hardman's answer is that this is "a parameter choice, not a change of primitive, and one KERI's agility accommodates by design" (same line) — which is an agility argument rather than a defence of the number, and is his assessment rather than a surveyed result. Two things follow that this chapter does not know and does not guess: whether KERI's and CESR's digest code points can express a 384-bit pre-rotation commitment in the `n` field without a specification change, and whether any deployed KERI ecosystem uses a digest other than Blake3-256. Against that, ENISA's certification guidance cuts the other way on the more basic question of whether to trust hashes at all, treating hash-based mechanisms as usable standalone while requiring the new lattice schemes to be combined with a classically secure mechanism (`kspqs.md` §The Pre-Rotation Mechanism, L72 @`181569b64`, reporting ENISA's *Agreed Cryptographic Mechanisms* v2.0) — a third-party rule, not Hardman's claim, and the strongest external support in the corpus for KERI's hash-first bet. The honest summary is that the *primitive* choice is externally vindicated and the *parameter* is externally questioned.

**Exactly what is checked at rotation.** keripy's `Kever.exposeds` is the firewall in code (`eventing.py:2940-2984`). For each rotation signature it takes the prior next-key digest at the signature's `ondex`, recomputes `Diger(ser=siger.verfer.qb64b, code=diger.code).qb64` over the newly revealed public key, and requires it to equal the committed digest (`eventing.py:2980-2982`). Only signatures whose revealed key hashes to a committed digest are counted. Then the exposed keys must satisfy the *prior* next threshold: `if not self.ntholder.satisfy(indices=ondices):` -> escrow plus `MissingSignatureError` "Failure satisfying prior nsith" (`eventing.py:2853-2863`). A rotation is therefore valid only if the newly revealed keys both hash to the previously committed digests *and* satisfy the prior next threshold -- "An attacker holding only current signing keys cannot rotate -- they do not know the pre-images." (raw/09 §2) The dual index is the enabling mechanism: keripy's `Siger` carries both `.index` (into current keys) and `.ondex` (into prior-next digests) (`eventing.py:2942-2984`). Rotation events therefore need up to two indices per signature -- "Second index looks up prior-next digest, first index the exposed key, verify digest, then verify signature." (KERI spec, L1256) A subtle cross-implementation rule: `ondex` must be computed from the *prior* establishment event, not the proposed one (signify-ts issue #378). (raw/10 §6d)

**The implementation defect a reviewer must guard.** The `exposeds` check can go *permissive* on a digest-code mismatch. Because the revealed key is re-digested using the *committed* digest's code, a wrong or mismatched code causes the recomputed digest not to match, and the `ondex` is "silently excluded from threshold satisfaction rather than failing closed -- weakening the pre-rotation check (the firewall, C1)." (`31-landmines.md` L6, flagged HIGH) A companion defect sits one layer up: `Serder._compute` overwrites the SAID field from raw bytes and "a wrong digest code yields an invalid SAID with no error." (`31-landmines.md` L7, HIGH) The standing invariant across both is "Never weaken or short-circuit this." (`30-invariants.md` C1)

**The scope of those two, said plainly, because it is the honest bound on them.** Both are knowledge-base observations at pin `9489ac9bd`, whose code baseline is keripy `60ab9e08` — 493 commits behind `b8f60166b`, and a `2.0.0-dev6` line against keripy's current `2.1.0-dev1`. Neither was re-tested at `b8f60166b` this cycle, and `core/eventing.py` and `core/serdering.py`'s `_compute` were not re-read. They stand as **discipline** — a reviewer touching this code should assume the permissive path is still there until they have looked — and not as a statement about what keripy does today. Any new digest code widens this surface, which is why the discipline matters; but the specific hazard once written up as an imminent Falcon problem is forward-looking rather than current, because keripy contains no post-quantum codes at all at `b8f60166b` (section 3).

## 6. Rotation and the general pre-rotation family

A basic rotation reveals the previously committed next keys as the new current keys, signs with them, and commits to a fresh next set. But the spec defines a whole family of rotations, and a reviewer should know they exist because they encode governance patterns that simpler systems cannot express.

- **Partial rotation** holds some pre-committed next keys in reserve rather than promoting all of them; **augmented rotation** adds new keys that were not pre-committed. In both cases "A rotation's current key list MUST be satisfiable w.r.t. BOTH prior-next and current thresholds." (KERI spec, §General Pre-rotation, L1470-1498) keripy enforces both thresholds jointly (the `exposeds`/`ntholder` check above plus the current `tholder` check).
- **Reserve rotation** lets reserve keypairs contribute to availability and fault tolerance without participating unless a non-reserve member is unavailable -- the basis for "provisional/custodial/escrow control authority." (KERI spec, §Reserve Rotation, L1501-1543)
- **Custodial rotation** is the sharpest governance primitive: a custodian holds signing authority (the current keys) while the owner holds *exclusive* rotation authority (the next keys), with the exposed rotation keys given weight 0 in the new current threshold. This lets the owner "revoke custodian's signing authority unilaterally." (KERI spec, §Custodial Rotation, L1545-1576) This exact pattern is what KERIA's passcode recovery uses (Section 12).
- **Abandonment** is a rotation to an empty next list, after which no further events are allowed. (KERI spec, L340, L174)

**The post-quantum recovery variants.** SQAR (Surprise Quantum Attack Recovery) is a partial-plus-augmented rotation to post-quantum-safe signing keys; it assumes the current signing keys and the prior-next keys are not PQ-safe but the prior-next *digests* are, and it gives the rotation keys weight 0 in the new current signing threshold (rotation authority only). (KERI spec, §SQAR, L1578-1606) The hybrid-multisig hedge is complementary: a threshold requiring both an ECC signature and a PQC signature means "Even if the ECC key is broken, the attacker cannot satisfy the full threshold without also breaking the post-quantum key." (`keri-primer.md` §2.5; raw/05 L89) Crypto agility makes this a per-identifier event, not an infrastructure project: "The upgrade is an event in the KEL. Not a global infrastructure project." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §6)

**The keystore hazard.** keripy advances and erases key material *before* the rotation event is validated: `mgr.rotate()`/`replay(advance=True)` mutate `PreSit` (old -> new -> nxt) and erase old private keys before `Kever.processEvent` validates the event. "On rejection you need an explicit `replay(advance=False)` recovery; a naive caller can strand the keystore ahead of the KEL." (`31-landmines.md` L11; `30-invariants.md` G2) This is an operational, not a protocol, hazard, but it is exactly the kind of thing that bites custodial deployments.

## 7. Multisig: exposing governance, not inventing it

KERI's multisig is fractionally weighted thresholds declared in the public KEL. The `k` list holds the public keys; the threshold (`kt` for current, `nt` for next) may be a simple M-of-N hex integer or a list of rational-fraction clauses. (KERI spec, L327) Clauses are ANDed; a clause is satisfied when its verified-signature weights sum to at least 1; rational fractions (not floats) avoid rounding errors. (KERI spec, L1419) `[1/2,1/2,1/2]` means "2 of 3." (KERI spec, L1421) Nested weighted lists model multi-device contributors. (KERI spec, L1466)

The corporate-board worked example makes the expressiveness concrete: three founders and four investors, policy "majority of Founders OR supermajority of Investors," encodes as `kt = [["1/2","1/2","1/2"],["1/3","1/3","1/3","1/3"]]` -- the two subarrays are clauses joined by OR, so any two founders (0.5x2=1.0) or any three investors satisfy it. (`keri-primer.md` §2.4; raw/05 L138, L149) Break-glass recovery encodes as Phone(0.5)+Laptop(0.5)+ColdStorage(1.0) at threshold 1.0: lose the phone and the cold-storage key rotates unilaterally. "The recovery logic is baked into the math of the identifier itself." (raw/05 L150)

The doctrinal point is that KERI's contribution here "is less about inventing and more about exposing." (`keri-primer.md` §2.4) Multisig is optional but declaring and following the policy is required: "multisig is optional and infinitely variable, but defining and following policy about it is required." (`keri-primer.md` §2.4; raw/05 L99) The papers ground this in millennia of practice -- Hammurabi's law 7, Deuteronomy 19:15, double-entry accounting -- and ask why anyone imagines "it's safe to use the single-signature mechanism of X509 certs as the only gate on unlocking the entire reputation of modern corporations." (`x509-prob.md` §One key isn't realistic; raw/06 §3)

In keripy, a multisig controller is `GroupHab`; the "local" member signs at the edge and contributes indexed signatures. A landmine: the indexed-signature code silently switches encoding at index >= 63 (`Ed25519_Crt_Sig` vs `Ed25519_Big_Crt_Sig`), so "A group/rotation with >63 keys changes the Siger encoding; consumers that don't handle the 'big' indexed codes may reject." (`31-landmines.md` L14) The "Joint issuance" work extends multisig toward "a weighted threshold of endorsements, each owner signing from their own identifier, asynchronously and with no shared key" -- "lighter and composable." (`sda.md` §8; raw/06 §6)

## 8. Delegation: cooperative, two-event, two-way peg

Delegation in KERI is cooperative, and the cooperation is what gives it its distinctive security property. A delegation is *two* events that reference each other: a delegating event in the delegator's KEL that contains a seal of the delegated event, and a delegated event (`dip` or `drt`) in the delegatee's KEL that contains the delegator's AID in its `di` field. "Both MUST participate." (KERI spec, §Cooperative Delegation, L1610-1612) The delegatee's AID is the digest of its `dip` event, which itself references the delegator's AID, so the delegatee is cryptographically bound to its delegator. (KERI spec, L1616) The papers call this contain-by-construction: a delegate's "very identifier commits to its delegator," so forbidden acts are "not merely against the rules but outside the space of possible moves." (`sda.md` §7; raw/06 §6)

**The joint-compromise property.** The delegator retains establishment control authority; the delegatee gets "revokable signing authority." (KERI spec, L1618) The security consequence is that compromising the delegatee alone is not enough: "any exploiter that merely compromises only the delegate's authoritative keys may not capture the control authority... A successful exploiter must also compromise the delegator's authoritative keys." Conversely, compromising only the delegator's signing keys cannot force a delegated rotation without the delegatee's pre-rotated keys. "Both sets of keys must be compromised simultaneously." (KERI spec, L1622) This is why delegated events carry an *extra* layer against dead and live attacks: an attacker who compromises the delegatee's pre-rotated keys still needs the delegator's anchoring seal, so it "must either induce the delegator to issue a seal or must also compromise the delegator's signing keys." (KERI spec, L1688-1690)

**Config traits `[N]`.** `DID` (Delegate-Is-Delegator) treats the delegatee as equivalent to the delegator for horizontal signing scale; `DND` (Do-Not-Delegate) forbids delegation. Delegation seals carried in interaction events are less secure than those in rotation events, because `ixn` is less secure than `rot`. (KERI spec `main`, L379-381 @`fbdd4a615`) On the standardizing line that is very nearly the whole of it: `DID` gets one sentence, which says the trait "enables the Controller to signal to validators that any Delegate (Delegatee) AIDs are to be treated as equivalent to the Delegator," plus the purpose clause "This enables horizontal scaling of a Delegator's signing infrastructure," and a table row reading "Treat a delegated AID the same as its Delegator AID" with Inception Only = True. (KERI spec `main`, L373, L381 @`fbdd4a615`) That sentence is correct and it is incomplete; `main` states **no placement rule for `DID` at all**, and everything in the next paragraph is absent from it.

**What `v1.1` adds, and it is the single largest doctrinal difference between the branches `[N1.1]`.** The `v1.1` branch replaces that one sentence with a nine-sentence rationale, all of it at §Configuration traits field, L381 @`3753f3797` unless noted. **Placement becomes normative:** the trait "appears in the Delegated Inception, `dip` event of a Delegatee and MUST NOT appear in a non-delegated Inception, `icp` event," and the table row now ends "Delegated inception only" (v1.1 L373). **What is being asserted is identity of entity, not of authority:** the trait signals "that the Controller of the Delegatee... and the Controller of the Delegator designated by the `di` field... are one and the same entity," where the Delegatee's controller is "the entity that holds the private keys." **Consent is the existing anchoring seal, with no new mechanism:** because "a Delegator MUST approve a Delegatee's delegated inception event by anchoring a delegation seal to that event in the Delegator's own KEL," the Delegator "thereby also consents to the appearance of the `DID` config trait in that event" — so the assertion is two-sided by construction, reusing cooperative delegation rather than adding to it. **The assertion is granular and does not propagate:** it "applies only to the delegation relationship established by the event in which it appears," and "It says nothing about any Delegatees that the Delegatee may itself delegate," each of which "makes or withholds the assertion in its own delegated inception event." **The placement rule has a stated reason, and it is referent ambiguity:** "Were the trait to appear instead in a Delegator's inception event, its referent would be ambiguous," because "a Delegator MAY itself be the Delegatee of a higher-level Delegator." **And the purpose is sharpened:** the trait "enables horizontal scaling of a Controller's signing infrastructure by making the equivalence of the AIDs that Controller operates verifiable from their KELs" — note the shift from `main`'s "a Delegator's" to "a Controller's," and the added claim that the equivalence is *derivable from the KELs* rather than merely asserted. On both branches `DID` remains Inception Only = True (L373), so it cannot be introduced by a later rotation.

**The boundary neither branch draws, which a reviewer should carry as the more important fact.** Both branches say a validator *may* treat the AIDs as equivalent. **Neither says what a validator MUST or MUST NOT do with that equivalence.** Nothing states whether a signature made under the Delegatee's key state satisfies a policy written against the Delegator's AID; nothing states whether the equivalence survives the Delegatee's own subsequent rotations; nothing states what happens when the Delegator later revokes or supersedes the delegation. The `v1.1` addition sharpens the *warrant* for the assertion — it grounds consent in the anchoring seal and makes the equivalence KEL-derivable — without specifying its *effect*. That is a gap rather than a contradiction, and it is the kind of gap that gets filled by implementation convention if no one fills it normatively. Compounding it: **no example anywhere shows `DID` in a `dip`.** Fay's delegated inception carries `"c": []` on both branches (`main` L781, `v1.1` L780), so `v1.1` describes the trait's correct usage at length and demonstrates it nowhere, and an implementer has no serialization to check against.

**A tension to record rather than resolve.** `main`'s own worked example places `DID` in exactly the position `v1.1`'s normative text forbids. Ean is a **Delegator**, not a Delegatee, and at `fbdd4a615` Ean's plain `icp` carries `"c": ["DID"]` (L591), with the prose "the config trait list for Ean has the config trait `DID`... Validators may treat Delegates (Delegatees) of Ean as if they were Ean" (L619). At `3753f3797` Ean's trait list is `[]` and the prose is rewritten to say so: "Ean's config trait list is empty; Ean is the Delegator rather than the Delegatee of the delegation shown in the examples that follow," so "the `DID` config trait, which belongs in a Delegatee's Delegated Inception, `dip` event, has no referent here." (v1.1 L618) There are two readings. Either `main`'s example is a defect that the `v1.1` rewrite is fixing, or the branches genuinely disagree about what `DID` means. The rewrite reads as a correction and the example was changed in the same breath as the rationale, which is evidence for the first — but **no source in this corpus says so**, only the inference, and the mining note declines to call `main` wrong. This chapter carries both readings and adjudicates neither. Do not state that `main`'s example is in error; state that the branches differ and which text each rests on. One incidental confirmation that the change was deliberate rather than incidental: removing `"DID"` shortens Ean's inception serialization by exactly five bytes, the version string records the drop from 681 to 676, and because Ean's AID is the SAID of that event, every AID literal, `p`, `di` and seal value downstream differs between the branches. Never cite an AID literal from the worked examples without naming the branch.

**What keripy actually enforces.** A delegated event is authorized only by a seal in the *delegator's* KEL; validation locates the anchoring event via the seal-source couple `(delseqner = sn, delsaider = SAID)` in `Kever.validateDelegation`. (`30-invariants.md` D1) The delegating event must be first-seen in the delegator's KEL before the delegated event is accepted, checked against `db.fons`. (`30-invariants.md` D2) The firewall rule is stated in the docstring: "The delegator MUST NOT accept a delegable event unless it is locally sourced, fully signed by its controller, and fully witnessed by its controller's designated witness pool." (`eventing.py:3087-3089`) And the delegator's approval logic is bound to witness changes: it "SHOULD NOT automatically approve a delegable rotation event unless that event's change to the witness pool is below the witness pool's prior threshold" (KERI spec, L1778) -- because a compromised delegatee could otherwise rotate in its own witnesses to bypass local security; the mitigation is a delegator time window so the delegate can *detect* the compromised superseding rotation. (`eventing.py:3099-3104`, `:3225-3238`)

**The gaps.** Three delegation landmines are live in keripy: `validateDelegation` "trusts `db.fons` even if the delegating event is only escrowed," which can falsely validate a delegate (`31-landmines.md` L13); delegated inception "has no confirmation loop; assumes delegator KEL already present," so a late-arriving delegator anchor can strand the dual-seal out of order (`31-landmines.md` L12); and a validator must be independently OOBI'd with the delegator before it can validate a delegate's KEL, because "A witness is NOT a watcher" -- witnesses store and serve but do not vouch for cross-AID anchors. (`30-invariants.md` D3; `31-landmines.md` L4)

## 9. Witness and backer designation

Witnesses are the availability and accountability layer, and a reviewer's single most common error is to read them as consensus nodes.

**What a witness is.** A witness is an entity "DESIGNATED (trusted) by the controller" that verifies, signs receipts for, and keeps events; it is the controller of its own non-transferable AID and is "Under ultimate control of the AID's controller," who may change the witness pool at will. (KERI spec, termdefs; L43) "A witness makes no such assertion. A witness simply stores and serves events." (`keri-primer.md` §2.6) The trust model toward witnesses is adversarial: "witnesses are assumed to be potentially malicious." (`keri-primer.md` §2.6)

**TOAD is not consensus.** The witness threshold is the Threshold Of Accountable Duplicity. In keripy the object is literally named for it: "`toader (Number): instance of TOAD (threshold of accountable duplicity)`" (`eventing.py:1945`). The name is the doctrine: "The witness threshold is literally named for duplicity accountability, not for consensus/finality. Witnesses provide accountability, not global ordering." (raw/09 §2) The controller declares the tally M in inception config, and "the controller accepts accountability for an event when any subset M of the N witnesses confirms that event." (KERI spec, L1846, L1850) KAWA (KERI's Algorithm for Witness Agreement) is a single-phase agreement that gives fault tolerance "without the multi-phase commit overhead of classical Byzantine Fault Tolerant algorithms like PBFT." (`cr-ad-trust.md` §4) Critically, its consistency guarantee is *local to the witness set*, not global across validators, and it does not bind a malicious controller who "can simply run its own witnesses." (`background.md` §5.8; raw/07 §2.4)

**Backers.** A backer is the ledger-backed alternative to a witness -- commonly a DLT-based registrar that anchors key events (or their SAIDs) on a ledger, requiring the `RB` config trait and a Registrar Backer Seal. (KERI spec, termdefs; L345, L494) A registry may even have zero backers: keripy's NoBackers (`NB`) trait raises `ValueError` if any backers are specified (`vdr/eventing.py:89-90`), directly contradicting the "witness = blockchain validator" prior.

**Local versus remote: the firewall as second factor.** keripy processes every event as local (protected) or remote (unprotected). "A local event may assume that the event only came via a protected transmission path... A remote event is received in an unprotected manner." (`eventing.py:3043-3047`) The purpose is "to allow increased security on local events where a threshold structure is imposed" (`eventing.py:3048-3049`): each witness only accepts local events, making "the controller's signature(s) the first factor and the set of unique witness factors a secondary threshold factor." An attacker must therefore compromise "not merely the controller's private key(s) but also the unique second factor on each of a threshold satisfycing number of witnesses." (`eventing.py:3051-3057`) The code even strips remotely sourced signatures from locally-membered indices before counting the threshold, "So that attacker can't source remotely compromised but locally membered signatures to satisfy threshold." (`eventing.py:2780-2791`)

**Witness invariants and one defect.** TOAD bounds: a non-empty witness list requires `1 <= toad <= len(wits)`; an empty list requires `toad == 0`. (`30-invariants.md` F1; `eventing.py:2869-2896`) Witness-set changes have their own rules: no duplicates, cuts must be a subset of current witnesses, and cuts and adds must be disjoint; removes are processed before adds; ordered-set math preserves witness index stability for indexed receipts. (`eventing.py:2696-2726`, `:2661-2665`; KERI spec L358, L362) The spec rule for sufficiency is "receipts meeting TOAD, not all witnesses" (`30-invariants.md` F2) -- but keripy's `WitnessReceiptor` "currently waits for ALL witnesses instead," a defect (not the invariant) that blocks indefinitely if a single witness is down and is the "Likely root of open twins #855 and #912." (`31-landmines.md` L9)

## 10. Recovery (superseding) rotation

Recovery rotation is the one narrow exception to first-seen dominance, and understanding it is understanding how KERI reconciles "first seen, always seen, never unseen" (KERI spec, L49) with the need to recover from compromise.

The base invariants are that at most one valid KEL exists per AID or none at all (KERI spec, L178), and that first-seen events are permanent. But if a current signing key is compromised, the controller needs to sign a *conflicting* event at an already-seen sequence number to reclaim control. Superseding recovery permits this under tight rules. keripy's own docstring: "Supersede means that after an event has already been accepted as first seen into a KEL that a different event with the same sequence number is accepted that supersedes the pre-existing event... This enables the recovery of events signed by compromised keys." (`eventing.py:3139-3142`)

**The forked KEL is preserved, not erased.** "the KEL is forked at the sn of the superseding event. All events in the superseded branch of the fork still exist but, by virtue of being superseded, are disputed." (`eventing.py:3143-3145`) The superseding fork is the authoritative branch; the superseded events "still remain in the KEL and may be viewed in order of their original acceptance." (`eventing.py:3148-3150`) This is duplicity-evidence by construction. The first-seen ordinal `fn` is distinct from `sn`: "Each event accepted into a KEL has a unique fn but multiple events due to recovery forks may share the same sn." (`eventing.py:3153-3155`)

**The superseding rules.** (KERI spec, L1804-1825; `eventing.py:3158-3207`)
- **A0:** a rotation may supersede an interaction at the same `sn` (provided that interaction is not before another rotation).
- **A1:** a non-delegated rotation may NOT supersede another rotation at the same `sn`.
- **A2:** an interaction may never supersede any event.
- **B:** a delegated rotation may supersede the latest-seen delegated rotation at the same `sn` under conditions governed by the delegator's KEL ordering (B1/B2/B3).
